I just can't seem to settle down, even when I'm trying to do something quiet, like... painting, or yoga, or sitting in a planning meeting at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I suppose... sometimes. At work, mostly. Over the past couple of years, I'll think I’ve checked a vendor spreadsheet carefully... but then our planning coordinator catches something. Like I transposed a SKU, or... missed a freight surcharge. Or maybe I carried the wrong quantity into the purchase order. Just... things like that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days when you felt unusually energized or on top of the world?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. I mean, I don't really get like that. Just... normal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot most days?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I worry about normal things, I guess. But... nothing out of the ordinary.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: I guess so. Um... mostly just when I'm on calls with suppliers. My foot is usually bouncing under the desk, and... I might be clicking a pen, or twisting my ring. Just doing it so much that I end up having to mute myself... or move my hands out of view. But... yeah. Just small things like that.\n\nDoctor: Did these attention and restlessness symptoms start before age 12?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I suppose so. I mean... back in elementary school, I'd get notes sent home sometimes. Just... for popping out of my seat, mostly. And... I guess I would talk over the other kids, or rush to answer things before the teacher even finished asking. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: I suppose so... sometimes. Um... like during vendor negotiations at work. Or... I do some STEM mentoring, too. I might... jump in with an answer before the other person is actually done talking. And then I realize I missed some important qualifier they were about to say. So... yeah. That happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty sustaining your attention when doing tasks that require prolonged focus?\n\nPatient: Um... I don't really think so. I mean... I seem to do okay with my work. I haven't really noticed a problem with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things needed for tasks or activities, like your keys, phone, or paperwork?\n\nPatient: Um... no. Not really. I mean... I usually know where my things are. I don't really have a problem with losing my keys or my phone, or anything like that. So... no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Um... sometimes, I guess. Over the past couple of years... I'll think I checked a vendor spreadsheet pretty carefully. But then... our planning coordinator will catch something. Like... I transposed a SKU, or... missed a freight surcharge. Or maybe I carried the wrong quantity into the purchase order. So... yeah, I suppose that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do these restlessness and impulsive behaviors cause problems for you in more than one part of your life, like at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Um... I mean, I guess so. At work... like I was saying, it just leads to corrected purchase orders, and... my planning coordinator having to do extra reviews. And at home... I don't know, family dinners can get kind of tense. They feel like I talk over them sometimes. So... yeah, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically unable to stay seated when you’re expected to remain seated?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I suppose. If a meeting runs a bit long... or we're having one of those tense dinners with my in-laws... I tend to find reasons to get up. Like... getting water, or checking the oven... or going to look at samples. I just... don't really like staying in my chair for all of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.
